Right, the checking. Yes, I just keep opening and closing the same drawers, and my wife gets very annoyed waiting for me to just finish up.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you're unable to complete your checking routines?\n\nPatient: Well, if I can't finish... I suppose I feel completely out of sorts. My stomach just ties up in knots. Like I was just saying a moment ago, I have to check the same boxes, my little lists, and those condo drawers over and over before I can move on with things. And if I'm interrupted and can't do it—even though I know perfectly well I already looked—I just can't relax. \n\nIt's especially hard when my wife is standing right there waiting for me to just leave it be. If she finally loses her patience and rushes me out the door, it ruins the whole car ride. We drive a Buick now, actually. Traded in the old Ford a couple of years ago because the seats were just murder on my lower back. The Buick has this adjustable lumbar support—marvelous invention, really. My son recommended it. He doesn't sell cars, he's in insurance, but he's always reading up on safety ratings and ergonomics and whatnot. He lives out in Ohio now, so we don't see him as much as we'd like... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, how I feel. Yes, it just bothers me terribly, like an itch I'm not allowed to scratch, if I have to walk away before I'm done looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have accidentally thrown away something important from my old teaching files. I taught middle school history for nearly forty years, you see. Kept all my lesson plans in these heavy metal filing cabinets. We had to move them all when we downsized to the condo, and you wouldn't believe how heavy those things are. The movers, these two young fellas, they were just huffing and puffing. I offered them some lemonade, but they just wanted water. Kids today, they're always carrying those enormous metal water bottles everywhere they go. My granddaughter has one covered in all these little stickers. She just started college out in Boston. Studying communications, whatever that means nowadays. When I was getting my bachelor's, you got a degree in English or History, not... well, anyway. \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. You were asking about my thoughts. Right. So, I get it in my head that I tossed a crucial file, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box when I was organizing. And even when I sit down in my chair and tell myself it's highly unlikely that I did that, the thought just comes right back. It just circles around and makes it terribly hard to settle down for the evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these repeating thoughts about throwing away something important are reasonable concerns?\n\nPatient: Well, reasonable? No, I suppose not. Like I mentioned a moment ago, I sit there in my chair and tell myself it's highly unlikely I actually did that. I know it doesn't make much sense. But the thought just comes back anyway, especially about my tools. I had this beautiful old set of wrenches. Craftsman, you know. Back when Sears was still open and they had that wonderful lifetime warranty. You could take a broken socket wrench in and the cashier would just hand you a brand new one, no questions asked. I used to go to the Sears at the Oakwood mall... gosh, that mall is an absolute ghost town now. They put in one of those indoor trampoline parks right where the appliance section used to be. Can you imagine? All these kids just bouncing off the walls where we used to buy our washing machines. My wife bought a lovely Kenmore washer there back in... oh, must have been '98. Lasted us twenty years, not like the junk they build today with all the computer chips in them that break after a year. \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? I apologize. You were asking if the concerns are reasonable. No, I know perfectly well it's unlikely I put a needed tool in the wrong box or tossed out a crucial teaching file by mistake. But knowing it's unreasonable doesn't seem to matter. That nagging thought just ignores logic, pops right back into my head, and makes it nearly impossible to settle down for the evening.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about something bad happening, like getting sick or causing harm, if you don't complete your checking routines?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I'm not worried about catching a disease or... or hurting anyone, no. It's just that awful uncomfortable feeling I mentioned, like an itch. It just feels *wrong* if I don't check. It's not about germs. Though, speaking of germs, my wife is always on me about using that hand sanitizer. She buys those little bottles from Bath & Body Works. The ones with the little rubber holders that hook onto her purse. They smell so strong, like... like fake vanilla or something. We went into that store once, and the smell just about knocked me over. It's right next to the pretzel stand at the mall—well, the other mall, the one that's still open. We get a pretzel there sometimes, split a large one with extra salt. The salt isn't great for my blood pressure, I know, my cardiologist, Dr. Evans, he's always telling me to watch the sodium. He's a good man, Dr. Evans. Plays golf with my brother-in-law down in Florida. They have these beautiful courses down there, but the humidity... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? You asked about bad things happening. No, I don't think I'll get sick or cause a disaster or anything like that. I just... I just have to do it so I can feel right and finally sit down.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about making sure everything is in its right place or is there something else that they're about?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it's just about those files and tools, like I was saying. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box. It's not about lining things up or making them perfectly straight, if that's what you mean. My neighbor, Tom, he's like that with his lawn. He mows it in these perfectly straight diagonal lines, like a baseball outfield. He uses one of those heavy John Deere riding mowers. Cost him a pretty penny, I'm sure. I always just used a push mower myself. Good exercise. Back when we had the house on Elm Street, I'd be out there every Saturday morning at eight o'clock sharp. The smell of fresh-cut grass, there's really nothing like it, is there? Reminds me of playing baseball when I was a kid. We used to play in this empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's grocery store. He'd come out and yell at us if we hit the ball too close to his windows... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts. Yes, it's just those thoughts about throwing away the files or misplacing the tools. Like I said, even when I sit there and tell myself it's highly unlikely I did it, the thought just comes right back and makes it so hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they more like worries that you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: Oh, they absolutely force their way in. I'm certainly not sitting there trying to worry on purpose! I'll just be trying to relax, and suddenly I get this... this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a needed tool in the wrong box. \n\nSpeaking of teaching, I used to keep all those files in these heavy metal cabinets. The olive green ones, you know? They weighed an absolute ton. We had them lined up in the teacher's lounge right next to the coffee maker. Awful coffee, just dreadful. We used that powdered creamer that didn't even need to be refrigerated. It was probably made of pure plastic, if you ask me. My colleague, Margaret, she used to bring in her own half-and-half, but she'd write her name on it in big black marker because the gym teacher, Coach Miller, would always steal it for his thermos. He drove this beautiful old Corvette, though. Cherry red. He spent every weekend waxing that thing... \n\nOh, listen to me going on about Margaret's creamer and Miller's car. I'm sorry, my mind just wanders. You were asking if the thoughts are intentional. No, not at all. Like I said, even when I sit there and firmly tell myself it's completely unlikely that I threw anything out or messed up my tools, the thought just pushes its way back in anyway. It just arrives uninvited and makes it so hard to settle down for the night, no matter how much I try to ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't care much about how things are arranged. Honestly, my desk in the den is a bit of a disaster area most days. It's this beautiful old oak roll-top desk I bought back in '82 from a furniture place downtown. That store used to be right next to the Rialto theater, the one with the big neon marquee. We used to go there on Sundays for the double features. Mostly Westerns back then. You know, John Wayne, Gary Cooper. They just don't make movies like that anymore. Everything now is just loud explosions and people flying around in spandex. My grandson, Timmy, he took me to one of those superhero pictures over Thanksgiving break. I swear, my ears were ringing for three days straight! The popcorn was good, though. They use that pump butter... well, they call it butter, but who knows what it actually is made of these days. Probably soy or something. \n\nAnyway, I apologize, I lost the thread there. You were asking about putting things in order? No, I don't have any rules about how things need to be lined up or arranged. It's really just the worrying about throwing away the files or misplacing my tools that bothers me, not the way they're sitting on the desk.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking habits and recurring thoughts affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are. I mean, unpacking this new condo has taken much, much longer than it really should have. Moving at my age is just a tremendous headache to begin with. The movers actually chipped the base of my favorite reading lamp. It's this beautiful heavy brass one I picked up during a trip to Charleston back in, oh, it must have been '94. We stayed in this wonderful little bed and breakfast right near the Battery. The woman who ran it, Mrs. Higgins I think her name was, she made the most incredible shrimp and grits for breakfast. I've tried making it myself over the years, but you really need proper stone-ground grits. You can't use that instant stuff they sell at the grocery store here. \n\nSpeaking of the grocery store, the one down the street just completely remodeled their layout. They moved the dairy section all the way to the opposite side of the building. It takes me twenty minutes just to find a carton of milk now! And the prices... I nearly had a heart attack looking at the price of eggs last Tuesday. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Rambling on about eggs. You asked if the checking is affecting my life. Yes, it is. Because unpacking the condo is taking so long with me getting stuck on these thoughts, I've been completely putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do down at the library. I haven't even touched my hobbies, either. I just feel like I absolutely can't move on to the tutoring prep or anything else until things are verified enough. So it really just stalls my whole day.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking habits cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's quite a bit of distress, honestly. It's just exhausting. I'll be trying to settle down with a cup of tea in the evening—Earl Grey, usually. I always buy the loose leaf kind from that little shop over on Elm Street. The owner there, Mr. Henderson, he's been running that place since I was a young teacher. He used to have this big orange tabby cat that would sleep right on the counter next to the cash register. The friendliest thing, it would just purr and purr while you paid. I think the cat's name was Marmalade. Or maybe it was Butterscotch? No, Butterscotch was my neighbor's dog back when I lived on Pine Avenue. A golden retriever, wonderful with kids. My daughter used to throw a tennis ball for him over the fence...\n\nOh, goodness, I'm sorry. I've wandered off again. Marmalade the cat has nothing to do with my worries. You asked about the distress. It's just... it's very upsetting. The thoughts just keep gnawing at me, and I get so anxious that I've made a terrible mistake with my files or tools. It just ruins my peace of mind, and I can't seem to shake the feeling no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking habits interfering with your ability to work or spend time with your family and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are getting in the way. Like I mentioned, unpacking this new condo has just taken ages. Much longer than it should have. It's funny, when I first started packing up the old house, my daughter Susan came over to help. She brought over these fantastic cardboard boxes she got from her office. She works in logistics, you know, over at that big shipping company near the interstate. She’s been there for fifteen years now, started right after she finished her degree. Anyway, she brought these boxes, and we ordered pizza from that Italian place on the corner of 5th and Main. Luigi's, I think it's called. Best pepperoni in town, though the crust has gotten a little thinner lately. I remember when Luigi himself used to toss the dough right there in the window. The kids used to just press their noses against the glass and watch him... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I apologize, my mind just wanders. You were asking about my work and family. I used to teach history, you know. Eighth grade, mostly. The curriculum changed so much over the years. I remember when we first got those new textbooks back in... oh, it must have been 1988. They had these glossy covers with a picture of the signing of the Declaration of Independence. The students actually liked those books, which was rare. One of my students, Tommy Richards, he went on to become a lawyer. I ran into his mother at the hardware store just last month. She was buying paint for her porch—a lovely shade of slate gray. I painted my own porch slate gray once, but the humidity here just peeled it right off after two summers. You really have to use a good primer if you want it to stick in this weather. My neighbor tried to tell me to use an oil-based one, but I... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry, I keep drifting off. You asked if I try to stop the thoughts. Yes, I do. It just makes it so incredibly hard to settle down and relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools are thoughts you want to have, or are they unwanted and intrusive?\n\nPatient: Well, they're certainly not thoughts I *want* to have. Goodness, no. They just pop in, completely uninvited. I'll be trying to read the paper, and suddenly I'm just consumed by this nagging feeling that I might have thrown out one of my old teaching files, or that I put a screwdriver in the wrong box. My father always swore by Craftsman. He was a carpenter, actually. Built half the houses in our old neighborhood. He had this massive wooden toolbox that he made himself, with all these little drawers and compartments. It smelled like sawdust and oil... just a wonderful smell. I mean, nobody's putting them in my head or anything like that. It's just my own brain going into overdrive. My brain has always been a bit hyperactive, I suppose. Well, I haven't really been seeing my friends or family much lately, to be honest. It's just getting in the way of everything. Unpacking the new condo has taken much, much longer than it ever should have. And I’ve been completely putting off my library tutoring preparation and my other hobbies. 

I do some volunteer tutoring at the public library, you see. It’s one of those beautiful old Carnegie libraries, built back in 1914, I believe. They have these magnificent solid oak reading tables. Real oak, not like the cheap veneer stuff they sell today. I bought a veneer coffee table once from a department store, and the edge chipped within a week. You just can't trust modern furniture to last. My grandfather actually made his own furniture. He had a lathe in his basement and he would turn these beautiful, intricate table legs. He made a dining set that my sister still has in her house in Ohio. She lives in Cleveland, right near the lake. The winters there are just absolutely brutal, though. I remember visiting her one January and the snow was piled all the way up to the mailboxes...

Oh, dear me. I'm drifting off again, aren't I? I am so sorry. My mind just goes for a walk without me. What was I saying? Oh, right, how it's affecting things. Yes, it's just that I feel like I can’t move on to any of my normal activities or go out and see people until things are verified enough. So I'm just stuck there, checking and re-checking, instead of getting on with my life.

No, it was Tuesday, because they sweep the streets on Wednesday and you have to move the car out of the visitor spots. We live in this condo complex now, we downsized a few years back. Right, the checking. Yes, I just keep opening and closing the same drawers, and my wife gets very annoyed waiting for me to just finish up.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you're unable to complete your checking routines?\n\nPatient: Well, if I can't finish... Like I was just saying a moment ago, I have to check the same boxes, my little lists, and those condo drawers over and over before I can move on with things. He doesn't sell cars, he's in insurance, but he's always reading up on safety ratings and ergonomics and whatnot. He lives out in Ohio now, so we don't see him as much as we'd like... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, how I feel. Yes, it just bothers me terribly, like an itch I'm not allowed to scratch, if I have to walk away before I'm done looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have accidentally thrown away something important from my old teaching files. I taught middle school history for nearly forty years, you see. Kept all my lesson plans in these heavy metal filing cabinets. We had to move them all when we downsized to the condo, and you wouldn't believe how heavy those things are. The movers, these two young fellas, they were just huffing and puffing. I offered them some lemonade, but they just wanted water. Kids today, they're always carrying those enormous metal water bottles everywhere they go. My granddaughter has one covered in all these little stickers. She just started college out in Boston. Studying communications, whatever that means nowadays. When I was getting my bachelor's, you got a degree in English or History, not... well, anyway. \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. You were asking about my thoughts. Right. So, I get it in my head that I tossed a crucial file, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box when I was organizing. And even when I sit down in my chair and tell myself it's highly unlikely that I did that, the thought just comes right back. It just circles around and makes it terribly hard to settle down for the evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these repeating thoughts about throwing away something important are reasonable concerns?\n\nPatient: Well, reasonable? No, I suppose not. Like I mentioned a moment ago, I sit there in my chair and tell myself it's highly unlikely I actually did that. I know it doesn't make much sense. But the thought just comes back anyway, especially about my tools. I had this beautiful old set of wrenches. Craftsman, you know. Back when Sears was still open and they had that wonderful lifetime warranty. You could take a broken socket wrench in and the cashier would just hand you a brand new one, no questions asked. I used to go to the Sears at the Oakwood mall... gosh, that mall is an absolute ghost town now. They put in one of those indoor trampoline parks right where the appliance section used to be. Can you imagine? All these kids just bouncing off the walls where we used to buy our washing machines. My wife bought a lovely Kenmore washer there back in... oh, must have been '98. Lasted us twenty years, not like the junk they build today with all the computer chips in them that break after a year. \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? I apologize. But knowing it's unreasonable doesn't seem to matter. That nagging thought just ignores logic, pops right back into my head, and makes it nearly impossible to settle down for the evening.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about something bad happening, like getting sick or causing harm, if you don't complete your checking routines?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I'm not worried about catching a disease or... or hurting anyone, no. It's just that awful uncomfortable feeling I mentioned, like an itch. It just feels *wrong* if I don't check. It's not about germs. Though, speaking of germs, my wife is always on me about using that hand sanitizer. She buys those little bottles from Bath & Body Works. The ones with the little rubber holders that hook onto her purse. They smell so strong, like... like fake vanilla or something. We went into that store once, and the smell just about knocked me over. It's right next to the pretzel stand at the mall—well, the other mall, the one that's still open. We get a pretzel there sometimes, split a large one with extra salt. The salt isn't great for my blood pressure, I know, my cardiologist, Dr. Evans, he's always telling me to watch the sodium. He's a good man, Dr. Evans. Plays golf with my brother-in-law down in Florida. They have these beautiful courses down there, but the humidity... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? You asked about bad things happening. No, I don't think I'll get sick or cause a disaster or anything like that. I just... I just have to do it so I can feel right and finally sit down.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about making sure everything is in its right place or is there something else that they're about?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it's just about those files and tools, like I was saying. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box. It's not about lining things up or making them perfectly straight, if that's what you mean. My neighbor, Tom, he's like that with his lawn. He mows it in these perfectly straight diagonal lines, like a baseball outfield. He uses one of those heavy John Deere riding mowers. Cost him a pretty penny, I'm sure. I always just used a push mower myself. Good exercise. Back when we had the house on Elm Street, I'd be out there every Saturday morning at eight o'clock sharp. The smell of fresh-cut grass, there's really nothing like it, is there? Reminds me of playing baseball when I was a kid. We used to play in this empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's grocery store. He'd come out and yell at us if we hit the ball too close to his windows... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts. Yes, it's just those thoughts about throwing away the files or misplacing the tools. Like I said, even when I sit there and tell myself it's highly unlikely I did it, the thought just comes right back and makes it so hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they more like worries that you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: Oh, they absolutely force their way in. I'm certainly not sitting there trying to worry on purpose! I'll just be trying to relax, and suddenly I get this... this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a needed tool in the wrong box. \n\nSpeaking of teaching, I used to keep all those files in these heavy metal cabinets. The olive green ones, you know? They weighed an absolute ton. We had them lined up in the teacher's lounge right next to the coffee maker. Awful coffee, just dreadful. We used that powdered creamer that didn't even need to be refrigerated. It was probably made of pure plastic, if you ask me. My colleague, Margaret, she used to bring in her own half-and-half, but she'd write her name on it in big black marker because the gym teacher, Coach Miller, would always steal it for his thermos. He drove this beautiful old Corvette, though. Cherry red. He spent every weekend waxing that thing... \n\nOh, listen to me going on about Margaret's creamer and Miller's car. I'm sorry, my mind just wanders. You were asking if the thoughts are intentional. No, not at all. Like I said, even when I sit there and firmly tell myself it's completely unlikely that I threw anything out or messed up my tools, the thought just pushes its way back in anyway. It just arrives uninvited and makes it so hard to settle down for the night, no matter how much I try to ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't care much about how things are arranged. Honestly, my desk in the den is a bit of a disaster area most days. It's this beautiful old oak roll-top desk I bought back in '82 from a furniture place downtown. That store used to be right next to the Rialto theater, the one with the big neon marquee. We used to go there on Sundays for the double features. Mostly Westerns back then. You know, John Wayne, Gary Cooper. They just don't make movies like that anymore. Everything now is just loud explosions and people flying around in spandex. My grandson, Timmy, he took me to one of those superhero pictures over Thanksgiving break. I swear, my ears were ringing for three days straight! The popcorn was good, though. They use that pump butter... well, they call it butter, but who knows what it actually is made of these days. Probably soy or something. \n\nAnyway, I apologize, I lost the thread there. You were asking about putting things in order? No, I don't have any rules about how things need to be lined up or arranged. It's really just the worrying about throwing away the files or misplacing my tools that bothers me, not the way they're sitting on the desk.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking habits and recurring thoughts affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are. I mean, unpacking this new condo has taken much, much longer than it really should have. Moving at my age is just a tremendous headache to begin with. The movers actually chipped the base of my favorite reading lamp. It's this beautiful heavy brass one I picked up during a trip to Charleston back in, oh, it must have been '94. We stayed in this wonderful little bed and breakfast right near the Battery. The woman who ran it, Mrs. Higgins I think her name was, she made the most incredible shrimp and grits for breakfast. I've tried making it myself over the years, but you really need proper stone-ground grits. You can't use that instant stuff they sell at the grocery store here. \n\nSpeaking of the grocery store, the one down the street just completely remodeled their layout. They moved the dairy section all the way to the opposite side of the building. It takes me twenty minutes just to find a carton of milk now! And the prices... I nearly had a heart attack looking at the price of eggs last Tuesday. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Rambling on about eggs. You asked if the checking is affecting my life. Yes, it is. Because unpacking the condo is taking so long with me getting stuck on these thoughts, I've been completely putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do down at the library. I haven't even touched my hobbies, either. I just feel like I absolutely can't move on to the tutoring prep or anything else until things are verified enough. So it really just stalls my whole day.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking habits cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's quite a bit of distress, honestly. It's just exhausting. I'll be trying to settle down with a cup of tea in the evening—Earl Grey, usually. I always buy the loose leaf kind from that little shop over on Elm Street. She’s been there for fifteen years now, started right after she finished her degree. Anyway, she brought these boxes, and we ordered pizza from that Italian place on the corner of 5th and Main. The kids used to just press their noses against the glass and watch him... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I apologize, my mind just wanders. You were asking about my work and family. I'll be sitting there, telling myself that it's just silly to worry about it, but I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files. They had these glossy covers with a picture of the signing of the Declaration of Independence. The students actually liked those books, which was rare. Well, yes, I suppose I did mention that a moment ago, didn't I? It's really putting a damper on things. Unpacking this new condo has just taken much, much longer than it ever should have. And I've been putting off my library tutoring preparation and my other hobbies, too. 

I used to do a lot of woodworking, you know. Nothing as grand as my grandfather's furniture, of course, just small things. Birdhouses, mostly. I made this lovely little bluebird box once out of cedar. Cedar is such a wonderful wood, it naturally repels insects. I bought the lumber from this old hardware store downtown, the kind with the creaky wooden floors and the smell of oil and sawdust. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he knew everything there was to know about wood. He had this enormous gray cat that just slept on the counter all day. I think its name was Barnaby. Barnaby would just open one eye when you walked in and then go right back to sleep. Cats have the right idea, don't they? Just sleep the day away in a sunbeam. My neighbor at my old house had a cat that used to leave "presents" on my porch. Usually mice, but once it was a rather large grasshopper...

Oh, my word. I am so sorry, doctor. I'm repeating myself and wandering off again. But yes, to answer your question, I feel like I just can't move on to any of my normal activities or see my family until things are verified enough. So the hobbies and the unpacking just sit there waiting while I check things.

She’s been there for fifteen years now, started right after she finished her degree. Anyway, she brought these boxes, and we ordered pizza from that Italian place on the corner of 5th and Main. The kids used to just press their noses against the glass and watch him... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I apologize, my mind just wanders. You were asking about my work and family. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. But mostly it's just... I have to do it to feel like I can finally stop. What brought me in, you see, is that I find myself checking the exact same boxes, the same lists, and the same drawers here in the new condo over and over again. I just can't seem to move on until I do it, even though I know perfectly well I've already looked. My wife, bless her, she's so patient, but she's always waiting for me to just leave it be so we can get on with our day. 

We've been married for forty-eight years now. We met at a county fair back in 1972. She was wearing this beautiful yellow sundress with little white daisies printed all over it. I won her a stuffed bear playing one of those ring-toss games. You know the ones, where the rings are always just a fraction too small for the bottles? It's a complete racket, really. The fellow running the booth had a gold tooth right in the front, and he kept winking at us. I remember we ate funnel cake afterward, and the powdered sugar got all over my dark blue trousers. I had to brush it off with a napkin, but it just smeared. It was a lovely day, though. We drove home in my old Ford Falcon. They don't make cars like that anymore, do they? All steel, none of this fiberglass and plastic nonsense. If you got into a fender bender back then, you just hammered it out and kept driving. My father had a Buick... 

Oh, for heaven's sake. Listen to me. I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry, doctor. My brain is just like a leaky faucet today. But yes, to get back to what you were asking, it's just that I keep checking the same drawers and boxes over and over, even when my wife is waiting for me to stop, just so I can feel that relief and finally move on.

No, I suppose not. Like I mentioned a moment ago, I sit there in my chair and tell myself it's highly unlikely I actually did that. I know it doesn't make much sense. Back when Sears was still open and they had that wonderful lifetime warranty. You could take a broken socket wrench in and the cashier would just hand you a brand new one, no questions asked. I'm not worried about catching a disease or... or hurting anyone, no. It's just that awful uncomfortable feeling I mentioned, like an itch. It just feels *wrong* if I don't check. It's not about germs. Though, speaking of germs, my wife is always on me about using that hand sanitizer. She buys those little bottles from Bath & Body Works. The ones with the little rubber holders that hook onto her purse. They smell so strong, like... like fake vanilla or something. We went into that store once, and the smell just about knocked me over. It's right next to the pretzel stand at the mall—well, the other mall, the one that's still open. We get a pretzel there sometimes, split a large one with extra salt. No, I don't think I'll get sick or cause a disaster or anything like that. I just... I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box. It's not about lining things up or making them perfectly straight, if that's what you mean. My neighbor, Tom, he's like that with his lawn. He mows it in these perfectly straight diagonal lines, like a baseball outfield. He uses one of those heavy John Deere riding mowers. Cost him a pretty penny, I'm sure. I always just used a push mower myself. Good exercise. Back when we had the house on Elm Street, I'd be out there every Saturday morning at eight o'clock sharp. The smell of fresh-cut grass, there's really nothing like it, is there? Reminds me of playing baseball when I was a kid. We used to play in this empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's grocery store. He'd come out and yell at us if we hit the ball too close to his windows... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts. Yes, it's just those thoughts about throwing away the files or misplacing the tools. Like I said, even when I sit there and tell myself it's highly unlikely I did it, the thought just comes right back and makes it so hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they more like worries that you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: Oh, they absolutely force their way in. I'm certainly not sitting there trying to worry on purpose! I'll just be trying to relax, and suddenly I get this... this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a needed tool in the wrong box. \n\nSpeaking of teaching, I used to keep all those files in these heavy metal cabinets. The olive green ones, you know? They weighed an absolute ton. We had them lined up in the teacher's lounge right next to the coffee maker. Awful coffee, just dreadful. We used that powdered creamer that didn't even need to be refrigerated. It was probably made of pure plastic, if you ask me. My colleague, Margaret, she used to bring in her own half-and-half, but she'd write her name on it in big black marker because the gym teacher, Coach Miller, would always steal it for his thermos. He drove this beautiful old Corvette, though. Cherry red. He spent every weekend waxing that thing... \n\nOh, listen to me going on about Margaret's creamer and Miller's car. I'm sorry, my mind just wanders. You were asking if the thoughts are intentional. No, not at all. Like I said, even when I sit there and firmly tell myself it's completely unlikely that I threw anything out or messed up my tools, the thought just pushes its way back in anyway. It just arrives uninvited and makes it so hard to settle down for the night, no matter how much I try to ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. The kids used to just press their noses against the glass and watch him... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I apologize, my mind just wanders. You were asking about my work and family. Well, because the unpacking is taking so long with all my checking, I've just been completely putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do down at the library. And I haven't even touched my hobbies. I just feel like I absolutely can't move on to the tutoring prep, or see my friends, or do anything else until things are verified enough. So, yes, it really is interfering with everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop these thoughts from coming into your mind, but they keep coming back anyway?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I certainly do try to stop them. I'll be sitting there, telling myself that it's just silly to worry about it, but I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files. I used to teach history, you know. Eighth grade, mostly. The curriculum changed so much over the years. I remember when we first got those new textbooks back in... oh, it must have been 1988. They had these glossy covers with a picture of the signing of the Declaration of Independence. The students actually liked those books, which was rare. One of my students, Tommy Richards, he went on to become a lawyer. I ran into his mother at the hardware store just last month. She was buying paint for her porch—a lovely shade of slate gray. I painted my own porch slate gray once, but the humidity here just peeled it right off after two summers. You really have to use a good primer if you want it to stick in this weather. My neighbor tried to tell me to use an oil-based one, but I... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry, I keep drifting off. You asked if I try to stop the thoughts. Yes, I do. But even when I tell myself it's unlikely that I threw away a file or put a needed tool in the wrong box, the thought just comes right back anyway. It just makes it so incredibly hard to settle down and relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools are thoughts you want to have, or are they unwanted and intrusive?\n\nPatient: Well, they're certainly not thoughts I *want* to have. Goodness, no. They just pop in, completely uninvited. I'll be trying to read the paper, and suddenly I'm just consumed by this nagging feeling that I might have thrown out one of my old teaching files, or that I put a screwdriver in the wrong box. Speaking of screwdrivers, I bought this wonderful set at Sears back when they still had that big store out at the mall. You remember the one? They had the best Craftsman tools. My father always swore by Craftsman. He was a carpenter, actually. Built half the houses in our old neighborhood. He had this massive wooden toolbox that he made himself, with all these little drawers and compartments. It smelled like sawdust and oil... just a wonderful smell. I used to sit in his workshop for hours watching him plane wood. He made me a little wooden birdhouse once, painted it blue...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? My mind is just a sieve today. You were asking if the thoughts are unwanted. Yes, very much so. Like I was saying, I'll keep getting these nagging thoughts about the files or the tools, and even when I try to tell myself it's completely unlikely that I threw anything important away, the thought just comes right back. It's incredibly intrusive, and it just makes it so terribly hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, they definitely feel like they're my own thoughts. I mean, nobody's putting them in my head or anything like that. It's just my own brain going into overdrive. My brain has always been a bit hyperactive, I suppose. Even when I was a boy, my mother used to say I had a motor that wouldn't quit. She was a wonderful woman, my mother. She used to bake these incredible apple pies every Sunday. She'd get the apples from Mr. Henderson's orchard just down the road. He had these beautiful Macintosh apples, so crisp and tart. He had a golden retriever named Buster who used to chase the tractors... \n\nOh, dear. There I go again. I'm so sorry, doctor. To answer your question, yes, they are my own thoughts. They just won't leave me alone, but they're definitely coming from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking habits affecting your relationships with family and friends in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really been seeing my friends or family much lately, to be honest. It's just getting in the way of everything. Unpacking the new condo has taken much, much longer than it ever should have. And I’ve been completely putting off my library tutoring preparation and my other hobbies. \n\nI do some volunteer tutoring at the public library, you see. It’s one of those beautiful old Carnegie libraries, built back in 1914, I believe. They have these magnificent solid oak reading tables. Real oak, not like the cheap veneer stuff they sell today. I bought a veneer coffee table once from a department store, and the edge chipped within a week. You just can't trust modern furniture to last. My grandfather actually made his own furniture. He had a lathe in his basement and he would turn these beautiful, intricate table legs. He made a dining set that my sister still has in her house in Ohio. She lives in Cleveland, right near the lake. The winters there are just absolutely brutal, though. I remember visiting her one January and the snow was piled all the way up to the mailboxes...\n\nOh, dear me. I'm drifting off again, aren't I? I am so sorry. My mind just goes for a walk without me. What was I saying? Oh, right, how it's affecting things. Yes, it's just that I feel like I can’t move on to any of my normal activities or go out and see people until things are verified enough. So I'm just stuck there, checking and re-checking, instead of getting on with my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. It's not about how they're arranged or needing them lined up perfectly or anything. Although, speaking of arranging things, when I was still teaching, I used to keep all my classroom library books arranged by historical era. Not alphabetically, mind you, but chronologically. The Renaissance right next to the Enlightenment. I had this wonderful set of historical encyclopedias I bought at an estate sale back in, oh, it must have been 1982. The estate belonged to a retired judge... Judge Carmichael, I believe his name was. Fascinating man. He had this magnificent mahogany desk with a green leather top in his study. Real leather, not that synthetic vinyl stuff they use now. It smelled faintly of pipe tobacco and old paper. My uncle Arthur used to smoke a pipe. Cherry wood, it was. He'd sit on the porch on summer evenings and blow smoke rings for us kids... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I am so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just get to talking and my mind wanders off down the street. To answer your question, no, I don't need things in a specific order to feel comfortable. It's really just the checking to make sure I haven't accidentally thrown the important things out. The order of them doesn't matter to me at all.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking habits making it difficult for you to enjoy activities or spend time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I did mention that a moment ago, didn't I? It's really putting a damper on things. Unpacking this new condo has just taken much, much longer than it ever should have. And I've been putting off my library tutoring preparation and my other hobbies, too. \n\nI used to do a lot of woodworking, you know. Nothing as grand as my grandfather's furniture, of course, just small things. Birdhouses, mostly. I made this lovely little bluebird box once out of cedar. Cedar is such a wonderful wood, it naturally repels insects. I bought the lumber from this old hardware store downtown, the kind with the creaky wooden floors and the smell of oil and sawdust. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he knew everything there was to know about wood. He had this enormous gray cat that just slept on the counter all day. I think its name was Barnaby. Barnaby would just open one eye when you walked in and then go right back to sleep. Cats have the right idea, don't they? Just sleep the day away in a sunbeam. My neighbor at my old house had a cat that used to leave \"presents\" on my porch. Usually mice, but once it was a rather large grasshopper...\n\nOh, my word. I am so sorry, doctor. I'm repeating myself and wandering off again. But yes, to answer your question, I feel like I just can't move on to any of my normal activities or see my family until things are verified enough. So the hobbies and the unpacking just sit there waiting while I check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself is what reduces your anxiety, or is it more about the relief you feel once you've confirmed everything is in order?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. But mostly it's just... I have to do it to feel like I can finally stop. What brought me in, you see, is that I find myself checking the exact same boxes, the same lists, and the same drawers here in the new condo over and over again. I just can't seem to move on until I do it, even though I know perfectly well I've already looked. My wife, bless her, she's so patient, but she's always waiting for me to just leave it be so we can get on with our day. \n\nWe've been married for forty-eight years now. We met at a county fair back in 1972. She was wearing this beautiful yellow sundress with little white daisies printed all over it. I won her a stuffed bear playing one of those ring-toss games. You know the ones, where the rings are always just a fraction too small for the bottles? It's a complete racket, really. The fellow running the booth had a gold tooth right in the front, and he kept winking at us. I remember we ate funnel cake afterward, and the powdered sugar got all over my dark blue trousers. I had to brush it off with a napkin, but it just smeared. It was a lovely day, though. We drove home in my old Ford Falcon. They don't make cars like that anymore, do they? All steel, none of this fiberglass and plastic nonsense. If you got into a fender bender back then, you just hammered it out and kept driving. My father had a Buick... \n\nOh, for heaven's sake. Listen to me. I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry, doctor. My brain is just like a leaky faucet today. But yes, to get back to what you were asking, it's just that I keep checking the same drawers and boxes over and over, even when my wife is waiting for me to stop, just so I can feel that relief and finally move on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the thoughts are certainly a nuisance. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, you know? Or that I've put a needed tool in the wrong box during the move. And even when I tell myself it's highly unlikely, the thought just comes right back and makes it terribly hard to settle down. 

Speaking of tools, I had this wonderful old Stanley hand plane that belonged to my father. It had a rosewood handle, smooth as glass from years of use. He used to sharpen the blade on this old oilstone he kept wrapped in a greasy rag in the garage. He could get that blade sharp enough to shave with, I swear it. He'd take a piece of rough oak and just peel off these paper-thin ribbons of wood. It smelled so good, like... like a forest after a rainstorm. We had this big oak tree in our front yard when I was a boy. Huge thing, must have been a hundred years old. My brother and I built a treehouse in it one summer. We used scrap wood we found behind the old lumberyard. My brother, Tommy, he was always the adventurous one. He climbed all the way to the top branches once and got stuck. The fire department had to come get him down. The fire chief, Mr. Higgins, he had this enormous mustache that covered his whole mouth...

Oh, dear me. I've done it again. I am so sorry, doctor. I just start picturing these things and my mouth runs away without me. But yes, the thoughts themselves are very bothersome. They just keep popping back into my head, telling me I've tossed out a file or misplaced a tool, and they just won't let me rest until I go check.

Even though I know perfectly well I already looked. \n\nIt's really trying my wife's patience, to be honest. She'll be standing by the door, just waiting for me to just leave it be so we can go. We were actually trying to get to the hardware store last Tuesday—or was it Wednesday? No, I suppose not. Like I mentioned a moment ago, I sit there in my chair and tell myself it's highly unlikely I actually did that. I know it doesn't make much sense. But the thought just comes back anyway, especially about my tools. I had this beautiful old set of wrenches. Craftsman, you know. Back when Sears was still open and they had that wonderful lifetime warranty. You could take a broken socket wrench in and the cashier would just hand you a brand new one, no questions asked. I used to go to the Sears at the Oakwood mall... gosh, that mall is an absolute ghost town now. They put in one of those indoor trampoline parks right where the appliance section used to be. Can you imagine? All these kids just bouncing off the walls where we used to buy our washing machines. My wife bought a lovely Kenmore washer there back in... oh, must have been '98. Lasted us twenty years, not like the junk they build today with all the computer chips in them that break after a year. \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? I apologize. You were asking if the concerns are reasonable. No, I know perfectly well it's unlikely I put a needed tool in the wrong box or tossed out a crucial teaching file by mistake. But knowing it's unreasonable doesn't seem to matter. That nagging thought just ignores logic, pops right back into my head, and makes it nearly impossible to settle down for the evening.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about something bad happening, like getting sick or causing harm, if you don't complete your checking routines?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I'm not worried about catching a disease or... or hurting anyone, no. It's just that awful uncomfortable feeling I mentioned, like an itch. It just feels *wrong* if I don't check. It's not about germs. Though, speaking of germs, my wife is always on me about using that hand sanitizer. She buys those little bottles from Bath & Body Works. The ones with the little rubber holders that hook onto her purse. They smell so strong, like... like fake vanilla or something. We went into that store once, and the smell just about knocked me over. It's right next to the pretzel stand at the mall—well, the other mall, the one that's still open. We get a pretzel there sometimes, split a large one with extra salt. The salt isn't great for my blood pressure, I know, my cardiologist, Dr. Evans, he's always telling me to watch the sodium. He's a good man, Dr. Evans. Plays golf with my brother-in-law down in Florida. They have these beautiful courses down there, but the humidity... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? You asked about bad things happening. No, I don't think I'll get sick or cause a disaster or anything like that. I just... I just have to do it so I can feel right and finally sit down.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about making sure everything is in its right place or is there something else that they're about?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it's just about those files and tools, like I was saying. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box. It's not about lining things up or making them perfectly straight, if that's what you mean. My neighbor, Tom, he's like that with his lawn. He mows it in these perfectly straight diagonal lines, like a baseball outfield. He uses one of those heavy John Deere riding mowers. Cost him a pretty penny, I'm sure. I always just used a push mower myself. Good exercise. Back when we had the house on Elm Street, I'd be out there every Saturday morning at eight o'clock sharp. The smell of fresh-cut grass, there's really nothing like it, is there? Reminds me of playing baseball when I was a kid. We used to play in this empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's grocery store. He'd come out and yell at us if we hit the ball too close to his windows... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts. Yes, it's just those thoughts about throwing away the files or misplacing the tools. Like I said, even when I sit there and tell myself it's highly unlikely I did it, the thought just comes right back and makes it so hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they more like worries that you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: Oh, they absolutely force their way in. I'm certainly not sitting there trying to worry on purpose! I'll just be trying to relax, and suddenly I get this... this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a needed tool in the wrong box. \n\nSpeaking of teaching, I used to keep all those files in these heavy metal cabinets. The olive green ones, you know? They weighed an absolute ton. We had them lined up in the teacher's lounge right next to the coffee maker. Awful coffee, just dreadful. We used that powdered creamer that didn't even need to be refrigerated. It was probably made of pure plastic, if you ask me. My colleague, Margaret, she used to bring in her own half-and-half, but she'd write her name on it in big black marker because the gym teacher, Coach Miller, would always steal it for his thermos. He drove this beautiful old Corvette, though. Cherry red. He spent every weekend waxing that thing... \n\nOh, listen to me going on about Margaret's creamer and Miller's car. I'm sorry, my mind just wanders. You were asking if the thoughts are intentional. No, not at all. Like I said, even when I sit there and firmly tell myself it's completely unlikely that I threw anything out or messed up my tools, the thought just pushes its way back in anyway. It just arrives uninvited and makes it so hard to settle down for the night, no matter how much I try to ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't care much about how things are arranged. Honestly, my desk in the den is a bit of a disaster area most days. It's this beautiful old oak roll-top desk I bought back in '82 from a furniture place downtown. That store used to be right next to the Rialto theater, the one with the big neon marquee. We used to go there on Sundays for the double features. Mostly Westerns back then. You know, John Wayne, Gary Cooper. They just don't make movies like that anymore. Everything now is just loud explosions and people flying around in spandex. My grandson, Timmy, he took me to one of those superhero pictures over Thanksgiving break. I swear, my ears were ringing for three days straight! The popcorn was good, though. They use that pump butter... well, they call it butter, but who knows what it actually is made of these days. Probably soy or something. \n\nAnyway, I apologize, I lost the thread there. You were asking about putting things in order? No, I don't have any rules about how things need to be lined up or arranged. It's really just the worrying about throwing away the files or misplacing my tools that bothers me, not the way they're sitting on the desk.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking habits and recurring thoughts affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are. I mean, unpacking this new condo has taken much, much longer than it really should have. Moving at my age is just a tremendous headache to begin with. The movers actually chipped the base of my favorite reading lamp. It's this beautiful heavy brass one I picked up during a trip to Charleston back in, oh, it must have been '94. We stayed in this wonderful little bed and breakfast right near the Battery. The woman who ran it, Mrs. Higgins I think her name was, she made the most incredible shrimp and grits for breakfast. I've tried making it myself over the years, but you really need proper stone-ground grits. You can't use that instant stuff they sell at the grocery store here. \n\nSpeaking of the grocery store, the one down the street just completely remodeled their layout. They moved the dairy section all the way to the opposite side of the building. It takes me twenty minutes just to find a carton of milk now! And the prices... I nearly had a heart attack looking at the price of eggs last Tuesday. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Rambling on about eggs. You asked if the checking is affecting my life. Yes, it is. Because unpacking the condo is taking so long with me getting stuck on these thoughts, I've been completely putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do down at the library. I haven't even touched my hobbies, either. I just feel like I absolutely can't move on to the tutoring prep or anything else until things are verified enough. So it really just stalls my whole day.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking habits cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's quite a bit of distress, honestly. It's just exhausting. I'll be trying to settle down with a cup of tea in the evening—Earl Grey, usually. I always buy the loose leaf kind from that little shop over on Elm Street. The owner there, Mr. Henderson, he's been running that place since I was a young teacher. He used to have this big orange tabby cat that would sleep right on the counter next to the cash register. The friendliest thing, it would just purr and purr while you paid. I think the cat's name was Marmalade. Or maybe it was Butterscotch? No, Butterscotch was my neighbor's dog back when I lived on Pine Avenue. A golden retriever, wonderful with kids. My daughter used to throw a tennis ball for him over the fence...\n\nOh, goodness, I'm sorry. I've wandered off again. Marmalade the cat has nothing to do with my worries. You asked about the distress. It's just... it's very upsetting. The thoughts just keep gnawing at me, and I get so anxious that I've made a terrible mistake with my files or tools. It just ruins my peace of mind, and I can't seem to shake the feeling no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking habits interfering with your ability to work or spend time with your family and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are getting in the way. Like I mentioned, unpacking this new condo has just taken ages. Much longer than it should have. It's funny, when I first started packing up the old house, my daughter Susan came over to help. She brought over these fantastic cardboard boxes she got from her office. She works in logistics, you know, over at that big shipping company near the interstate. She’s been there for fifteen years now, started right after she finished her degree. Anyway, she brought these boxes, and we ordered pizza from that Italian place on the corner of 5th and Main. Luigi's, I think it's called. Best pepperoni in town, though the crust has gotten a little thinner lately. I remember when Luigi himself used to toss the dough right there in the window. The kids used to just press their noses against the glass and watch him... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I apologize, my mind just wanders. You were asking about my work and family. Well, because the unpacking is taking so long with all my checking, I've just been completely putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do down at the library. And I haven't even touched my hobbies. I just feel like I absolutely can't move on to the tutoring prep, or see my friends, or do anything else until things are verified enough. So, yes, it really is interfering with everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop these thoughts from coming into your mind, but they keep coming back anyway?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I certainly do try to stop them. I'll be sitting there, telling myself that it's just silly to worry about it, but I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files. I used to teach history, you know. Eighth grade, mostly. The curriculum changed so much over the years. I remember when we first got those new textbooks back in... oh, it must have been 1988. They had these glossy covers with a picture of the signing of the Declaration of Independence. The students actually liked those books, which was rare. One of my students, Tommy Richards, he went on to become a lawyer. I ran into his mother at the hardware store just last month. She was buying paint for her porch—a lovely shade of slate gray. I painted my own porch slate gray once, but the humidity here just peeled it right off after two summers. You really have to use a good primer if you want it to stick in this weather. My neighbor tried to tell me to use an oil-based one, but I... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry, I keep drifting off. You asked if I try to stop the thoughts. Yes, I do. But even when I tell myself it's unlikely that I threw away a file or put a needed tool in the wrong box, the thought just comes right back anyway. It just makes it so incredibly hard to settle down and relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools are thoughts you want to have, or are they unwanted and intrusive?\n\nPatient: Well, they're certainly not thoughts I *want* to have. Goodness, no. They just pop in, completely uninvited. I'll be trying to read the paper, and suddenly I'm just consumed by this nagging feeling that I might have thrown out one of my old teaching files, or that I put a screwdriver in the wrong box. Speaking of screwdrivers, I bought this wonderful set at Sears back when they still had that big store out at the mall. You remember the one? They had the best Craftsman tools. My father always swore by Craftsman. He was a carpenter, actually. Built half the houses in our old neighborhood. He had this massive wooden toolbox that he made himself, with all these little drawers and compartments. It smelled like sawdust and oil... just a wonderful smell. I used to sit in his workshop for hours watching him plane wood. He made me a little wooden birdhouse once, painted it blue...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? My mind is just a sieve today. You were asking if the thoughts are unwanted. Yes, very much so. Like I was saying, I'll keep getting these nagging thoughts about the files or the tools, and even when I try to tell myself it's completely unlikely that I threw anything important away, the thought just comes right back. It's incredibly intrusive, and it just makes it so terribly hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, they definitely feel like they're my own thoughts. I mean, nobody's putting them in my head or anything like that. It's just my own brain going into overdrive. My brain has always been a bit hyperactive, I suppose. Even when I was a boy, my mother used to say I had a motor that wouldn't quit. She was a wonderful woman, my mother. She used to bake these incredible apple pies every Sunday. He had a golden retriever named Buster who used to chase the tractors... \n\nOh, dear. There I go again. I'm so sorry, doctor. To answer your question, yes, they are my own thoughts. They just won't leave me alone, but they're definitely coming from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking habits affecting your relationships with family and friends in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really been seeing my friends or family much lately, to be honest. It's just getting in the way of everything. Unpacking the new condo has taken much, much longer than it ever should have. And I’ve been completely putting off my library tutoring preparation and my other hobbies. \n\nI do some volunteer tutoring at the public library, you see. It’s one of those beautiful old Carnegie libraries, built back in 1914, I believe. They have these magnificent solid oak reading tables. Real oak, not like the cheap veneer stuff they sell today. I bought a veneer coffee table once from a department store, and the edge chipped within a week. You just can't trust modern furniture to last. My grandfather actually made his own furniture. He had a lathe in his basement and he would turn these beautiful, intricate table legs. He made a dining set that my sister still has in her house in Ohio. She lives in Cleveland, right near the lake. The winters there are just absolutely brutal, though. It's not about how they're arranged or needing them lined up perfectly or anything. Although, speaking of arranging things, when I was still teaching, I used to keep all my classroom library books arranged by historical era. Not alphabetically, mind you, but chronologically. The Renaissance right next to the Enlightenment. I had this wonderful set of historical encyclopedias I bought at an estate sale back in, oh, it must have been 1982. The estate belonged to a retired judge... Judge Carmichael, I believe his name was. Fascinating man. He had this magnificent mahogany desk with a green leather top in his study. Real leather, not that synthetic vinyl stuff they use now. It smelled faintly of pipe tobacco and old paper. My uncle Arthur used to smoke a pipe. Cherry wood, it was. He'd sit on the porch on summer evenings and blow smoke rings for us kids... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I am so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just get to talking and my mind wanders off down the street. To answer your question, no, I don't need things in a specific order to feel comfortable. It's really just the checking to make sure I haven't accidentally thrown the important things out. The order of them doesn't matter to me at all.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking habits making it difficult for you to enjoy activities or spend time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I did mention that a moment ago, didn't I? It's really putting a damper on things. Unpacking this new condo has just taken much, much longer than it ever should have. And I've been putting off my library tutoring preparation and my other hobbies, too. \n\nI used to do a lot of woodworking, you know. Nothing as grand as my grandfather's furniture, of course, just small things. Birdhouses, mostly. I made this lovely little bluebird box once out of cedar. Cedar is such a wonderful wood, it naturally repels insects. I bought the lumber from this old hardware store downtown, the kind with the creaky wooden floors and the smell of oil and sawdust. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he knew everything there was to know about wood. He had this enormous gray cat that just slept on the counter all day. I think its name was Barnaby. Barnaby would just open one eye when you walked in and then go right back to sleep. Cats have the right idea, don't they? Just sleep the day away in a sunbeam. So the hobbies and the unpacking just sit there waiting while I check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself is what reduces your anxiety, or is it more about the relief you feel once you've confirmed everything is in order?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. But mostly it's just... I have to do it to feel like I can finally stop. What brought me in, you see, is that I find myself checking the exact same boxes, the same lists, and the same drawers here in the new condo over and over again. I just can't seem to move on until I do it, even though I know perfectly well I've already looked. My wife, bless her, she's so patient, but she's always waiting for me to just leave it be so we can get on with our day. \n\nWe've been married for forty-eight years now. We met at a county fair back in 1972. She was wearing this beautiful yellow sundress with little white daisies printed all over it. I won her a stuffed bear playing one of those ring-toss games. You know the ones, where the rings are always just a fraction too small for the bottles? It's a complete racket, really. The fellow running the booth had a gold tooth right in the front, and he kept winking at us. I remember we ate funnel cake afterward, and the powdered sugar got all over my dark blue trousers. I had to brush it off with a napkin, but it just smeared. It was a lovely day, though. We drove home in my old Ford Falcon. They don't make cars like that anymore, do they? All steel, none of this fiberglass and plastic nonsense. If you got into a fender bender back then, you just hammered it out and kept driving. My father had a Buick... \n\nOh, for heaven's sake. Listen to me. I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry, doctor. My brain is just like a leaky faucet today. But yes, to get back to what you were asking, it's just that I keep checking the same drawers and boxes over and over, even when my wife is waiting for me to stop, just so I can feel that relief and finally move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about throwing away something important or misplacing tools are more bothersome to you than the act of checking itself?\n\nPatient: Well, the thoughts are certainly a nuisance. And even when I tell myself it's highly unlikely, the thought just comes right back and makes it terribly hard to settle down. \n\nSpeaking of tools, I had this wonderful old Stanley hand plane that belonged to my father. He could get that blade sharp enough to shave with, I swear it. He'd take a piece of rough oak and just peel off these paper-thin ribbons of wood. It smelled so good, like... like a forest after a rainstorm. We had this big oak tree in our front yard when I was a boy. Huge thing, must have been a hundred years old. My brother and I built a treehouse in it one summer. We used scrap wood we found behind the old lumberyard. My brother, Tommy, he was always the adventurous one. He climbed all the way to the top branches once and got stuck. The fire department had to come get him down. The fire chief, Mr. Higgins, he had this enormous mustache that covered his whole mouth...\n\nOh, dear me. I've done it again. I am so sorry, doctor. Well, I suppose it's more about the discomfort, really. I just get this awful, unsettled feeling, and I can't shake it. Like I mentioned, that's really what brought me in to see you.
